News Brief
Florida is set to hold a public hearing on October 8, 2026, in Lake Mary to discuss the potential removal of school vaccine requirements for chickenpox, hepatitis B, and two other vaccines. The hearing notice was published in the Florida Administrative Register on September 30, 2026, and the event is part of an ongoing review of vaccine mandates. The Florida Department of Health has not confirmed or denied the status of hepatitis B virus in the evidence provided. The hearing aims to gather public input from parents regarding the proposed changes, which could affect children enrolled in school or day care. No confirmed cases, deaths, or exposures related to hepatitis B virus are reported in the evidence. The hearing is a consultation process and does not provide definitive conclusions about the virus's impact or status.

Healthy Living Florida Sets October 8 Lake Mary Hearing on Dropping Chickenpox, Hepatitis B and Two Other School Vaccine Requirements Published Oct 02, 2026 9:50 PM EDT By Joseph James NIKOLAY DOYCHINOV | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on Pocket Developing Story Note: This report covers new developments involving Florida's proposed changes to school and child-care vaccine rules. Information may change as the Florida Department of Health releases additional findings. The newest information comes from a hearing notice the department published in the Florida Administrative Register on September 30, 2026, and was last checked on October 2, 2026. Florida parents now have a firm date to weigh in before the state decides whether children still need four vaccines to enroll in school or day care. The Florida Department of Health will hold a public hearing on October 8, 2026, from 9 a.m. to noon at the Orlando Marriott Lake Mary, according to a notice in the Florida Administrative Register. The hearing covers the department's plan to drop entry requirements for the chickenpox (varicella), hepatitis B, pneumococcal conjugate, and Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) vaccines. The rule has not taken effect, and nothing about the recommended childhood vaccine schedule changes because of it. The vaccines would remain available, but schools, preschools, daycares, and adult education programs could no longer require proof of them, as WUSF reported. That puts the decision squarely on families, many of whom rely on enrollment paperwork as a reminder of which shots their child is due for. The stakes may be greatest for households with babies and toddlers in group care. Hib and pneumococcal vaccines are given mainly in the first years of life, when those infections are most dangerous, so a day-care classroom, not a high school hallway, is where the rule change could matter first. A Lake Mary Ballroom Becomes the Last Public Stop The hearing will take place in Grand Ballroom E, F, G, and H of the hotel at 1501 International Parkway in Lake Mary, north of Orlando. The notice says the session will end at noon or when the hearing concludes, whichever comes first. People can request an agenda from Tom Bendle of the department's Division of Disease Control at Thomas.Bendle@flhealth.gov or 850-245-4342. The rule at issue, 64D-3.046 of the Florida Administrative Code, sets immunization paperwork for public and private schools from preschool through grade 12 and for adult education classes. The department also posted a notice of change to the proposed rule on September 30, though its online summary does not describe the edits. MedicalDaily first reported in August that Florida moved to drop four school vaccine requirements after Surgeon General Dr. Joseph Ladapo approved the proposal. At that time, the plan was open for written comments, which the proposed rule notice said would run from August 24 to September 14. Earlier this week, MedicalDaily also covered a measles modeling study that projected outbreak risks if school coverage fell further. The new development is the in-person hearing, the only public hearing announced so far. Ladapo's Rationale Meets Pediatricians' Warnings In its proposed rule notice, the department said the change "removes from requirements immunizations that are not required by section 1003.22, Florida Statutes." In practice, the state is keeping only the shots that lawmakers wrote into law. The measles, mumps, rubella, polio, diphtheria, tetanus, and whooping cough requirements stay because only the Legislature can remove them. The same proposal would let parents cite moral and ethical beliefs on the state's religious exemption form. Ladapo has framed the effort as a matter of medical freedom and parental choice. At a September 3, 2025, news conference in Valrico, he said of vaccine mandates, "Every last one of them is wrong and drips with disdain and slavery," according to a PolitiFact review that rated the comparison Pants on Fire, its lowest rating. The Florida Chapter of the American Academy of Pediatrics sees a different risk. "Even when exercised infrequently, nonmedical exemptions are neither harmless nor benign," the group said, adding that such exemptions "lead to lower immunization rates and weaken our ability as a state to protect ourselves against dangerous diseases." Doctors at a department workshop in December 2025 also raised concerns about meningitis, WUSF reported. Day-Care Families Face the First Practical Questions Florida's vaccine coverage has already slipped. Vaccination rates among the state's children have fallen nearly 5% since the 2019-20 school year, and only 88.8% of kindergartners had the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine in 2024-25, according to WUSF. That is below the national average of 93% and short of the 95% level linked to preventing measles outbreaks. Those numbers concern a vaccine that is not part of this rule, but they show the trend pediatricians fear could spread to the four shots now under review. Hib can cause meningitis, pneumonia, bloodstream infections, and throat swelling, mainly in children under 5, according to the CDC. The agency recommends the vaccine for all children in that age group. Children too young to finish their shots, and those with weakened immune systems, depend most on high coverage among classmates. Parents can take simple steps now. They can ask their pediatrician or county health department for their child's record and confirm whether any doses are overdue, since doctor recommendations have not changed. Families can also ask their day care or school how it will respond, since the proposal removes requirements but does not stop programs from encouraging vaccination. Cost should not be a barrier for many households. Most private insurance plans cover routine childhood vaccines, and the federal Vaccines for Children program supplies them at no cost to eligible children through county health departments and participating clinics. Parents with questions about a specific vaccine or their child's health should talk with a clinician. Adoption Timeline Remains Unsettled The department has not announced when it will file the rule for adoption, and no effective date has been set. Under Florida's rulemaking process, a rule generally takes effect 20 days after it is filed with the Department of State unless a later date is listed. It remains unclear whether the notice of change will alter the final version or whether a legal challenge will follow. MedicalDaily will track the hearing and any filing that follows. For now, the four vaccines remain required for Florida school and day-care entry, and doctors still recommend them on the usual schedule. Families who want a voice in the decision have one week to plan, and checking a child's vaccine record is a reasonable step whatever the outcome. Read more Kentucky's Measles Count More Than Doubles Since Mid-August to 46 with Four Hospitalized and No Recorded Vaccinations Liberals and Conservatives Each Doubted the Other Side's Religious Exemption Claims in a New Behavioral Science Study Pediatricians Widen the RSV High Risk List and More Toddlers Now Qualify for a Second Season Dose Key Questions Answered When and where is Florida's vaccine rule hearing? The Florida Department of Health will hold the hearing on October 8, 2026, from 9 a.m. to noon at the Orlando Marriott Lake Mary, 1501 International Parkway. It may end earlier if the hearing concludes before noon. Which vaccines would no longer be required? The proposal would remove school and child-care entry requirements for the chickenpox, hepatitis B, pneumococcal conjugate, and Hib vaccines. These shots would still be available and recommended by doctors. Are measles and polio shots still required in Florida schools? Yes. Measles, mumps, rubella, polio, diphtheria, tetanus, and whooping cough requirements are written into state law, so only the Florida Legislature can change them. Has the rule already taken effect? No. The hearing is still ahead, the department has not filed the rule for adoption, and no effective date has been announced. The four vaccines remain required for now. Who could be most affected if the rule is adopted? Infants and toddlers in day care face the greatest exposure risk, because Hib and pneumococcal infections are most dangerous in children under 5. Children with weakened immune systems and those too young to finish their vaccine series also rely on high coverage around them. What can parents do right now? Parents can review their child's vaccine record with a pediatrician or county health department, ask their school or day care how it plans to respond, and attend the hearing or contact the department to share their views. Accelerated Aging and Colon Cancer; Hepatitis B From a Cat? Food Triggers in IBD
新闻简报本新闻报道重点介绍了与 乙型肝炎病毒 (HBV) 相关的几项研究及其影响。一项全球研究发现,慢性肝衰竭急性患者的肝移植与 1 年 死亡率 显着降低相关。在韩国,一项大型 队列研究 研究将生物衰老加速与早发性结直肠腺癌风险增加 93% 和早发性结肠腺癌风险增加 132% 联系起来。一项中国研究提出了一种评估 2 型糖尿病患者胰腺癌风险的算法。此外,临床胃肠病学和肝病学的一项研究指出,家猫传播 HBV 传播 的风险很小。这些发现基于观察性和回顾性研究,没有提及明确的公共卫生反应或调查状态。
查看新闻五要素
时间
访问故事书了解更多信息。
地点与对象
韩国、中国和全球。
事件
一项研究表明,家猫感染人类的​​ 乙型肝炎病毒 传播 的边际风险,另一项研究发现,加速的生物衰老与韩国 队列研究 早发结直肠癌和结肠腺癌的风险增加有关。此外,来自中国的一项研究提出了一种评估 2 型糖尿病患者胰腺癌风险的算法,一项全球研究显示,肝移植治疗慢加急性肝衰竭的 1 年 死亡率 较低。
规模、影响与风险
韩国研究报告称,早发性结直肠腺癌的风险增加 93%,早发性结肠腺癌的风险增加 132%。中国的研究提出了一种胰腺癌风险评估的算法。全球肝移植研究显示 1 年 死亡率 显着降低。
应对、状态与不确定性
来自猫的 乙型肝炎病毒 传播 风险被认为是边际风险。其他研究是观察性或回顾性的,没有明确提及公共卫生反应或调查状态。研究结果基于临床和 队列研究 研究。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Accelerated Aging and Colon Cancer; Hepatitis B From a Cat? Food Triggers in IBD

News Brief
This news report highlights several studies related to hepatitis B virus (HBV) and its implications. A global study found that liver transplant for patients with acute-on-chronic liver failure was associated with markedly lower 1-year mortality. In Korea, a large cohort study linked accelerated biological aging with a 93% increased risk of early-onset colorectal adenocarcinoma and a 132% higher risk of early-onset colon adenocarcinoma. A Chinese study proposed an algorithm for assessing pancreatic cancer risk in type 2 diabetes patients. Additionally, there is a marginal risk of HBV transmission from domestic cats to humans, as noted in a study from Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology. These findings are based on observational and retrospective research, with no explicit public health response or investigation status mentioned.

Food Triggers in IBD — News and commentary from the world of gastroenterology and hepatology by Ian Ingram, Managing Editor, MedPage Today October 8, 2026 • 2 min read Add MedPage Today on Google MedpageToday In a large Korean cohort, accelerated biological aging was associated with a 93% increased risk of early-onset colorectal adenocarcinoma and a 132% higher risk of early-onset colon adenocarcinoma specifically. (Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology) An algorithm using routinely collected clinical parameters could offer insight into pancreatic cancer risk in type 2 diabetes patients, a study from China suggested. (Gut) Experts in Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology reviewed the diagnosis and management of acid-related disorders, including gastroesophageal reflux disease, peptic ulcer disease, and more. Anorectal biofeedback therapy and dextranomer-hyaluronate injections showed similar efficacy in a randomized study of refractory fecal incontinence. (Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology) Researchers suggested there's a marginal risk of hepatitis B virus transmission from domestic cats to humans. (Hepatology) In a phase II trial of chronic hepatitis C, an 8-week course of investigational bemnifosbuvir and ruzasvir led to sustained virologic response in 90% of patients at 12 weeks. (Hepatology) The American College of Gastroenterology issued new guidelines on preventive care in cirrhosis. (American Journal of Gastroenterology) Renin-angiotensin system inhibitors and calcium channel blockers may be liver protective for hypertensive patients with metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease. (Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology) Resmetirom's (Rezdiffra) efficacy in metabolic dysfunction-associated steatohepatitis (MASH) appeared to be independent of common MASH risk genotypes, according to an analysis of the drug's pivotal trial. (Journal of Hepatology) Liver transplant for patients with acute-on-chronic liver failure was associated with markedly lower 1-year mortality in a global study. (Journal of Hepatology) Sex differences in hepatocellular carcinoma mortality. (JAMA Network Open) Why do inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) patients experience symptoms from foods they previously tolerated? Researchers uncovered clues. (Gastroenterology) Mount Sinai announced NIH funding for a study to investigate whether per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances -- so-called forever chemicals -- play a role in IBD development. In a nationwide cohort study, early surgical intervention with colectomy in ulcerative colitis was linked with lower cardiometabolic disease risks later on. (American Journal of Gastroenterology) A network meta-analysis of advanced therapies as induction for ulcerative colitis backed upadacitinib (Rinvoq) for efficacy and anti-p19 biologics in terms of safety. (Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology) Meanwhile, a retrospective study comparing JAK inhibitors for acute severe ulcerative colitis found a higher rate of colectomy at 52 weeks with Tofacitinib (Xeljanz) than with upadacitinib (42.5% vs 23.4%). View five news elements
Time
Visit Storybook for more information.
Location and population
Korea, China, and globally.
Event
A study suggested a marginal risk of hepatitis B virus transmission from domestic cats to humans, and another study found that accelerated biological aging was associated with increased risks of early-onset colorectal and colon adenocarcinoma in a Korean cohort. Additionally, a study from China proposed an algorithm for assessing pancreatic cancer risk in type 2 diabetes patients, and a global study showed lower 1-year mortality with liver transplant for acute-on-chronic liver failure.
Scale, impact and risk
The Korean study reported a 93% increased risk of early-onset colorectal adenocarcinoma and a 132% higher risk of early-onset colon adenocarcinoma. The Chinese study suggested an algorithm for pancreatic cancer risk assessment. The global liver transplant study showed markedly lower 1-year mortality.
Response, status and uncertainty
The hepatitis B virus transmission risk from cats is noted as marginal. The other studies are observational or retrospective, with no explicit mention of public health response or investigation status. The findings are based on clinical and cohort studies.
